, we obtain a map

\displaystyle U_{i}

\displaystyle\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.68929pt\raise 6.30835pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.47499pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cap T_{i+1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.13838pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.27676pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{H}_{i+1,\psi(d,k)_{d-i}}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto(T_{1\cdots i}|_{x}\times_{U_{i}}\mathcal{L}|_{U_{i}})\times_{\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{U_{i}}}T_{i+1}|_{U_{i}}.

This completes the induction step. We have thus constructed a tower of maps

U_{d}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\cdots\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>U_{4}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>U_{3}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>U_{2}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>U_{1}=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathbf{a}}.

Further, from the inductive construction and Lemmas
4.10
and
5.3
, we have

\RD(U_{d}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathbf{a}})\leq\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}}).

Now let
\mathcal{L}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{H}_{d,\psi(d,k)_{1}}
denote the tautological
k
-plane bundle (n.b.
k=\psi(d,k)_{0}
). Then, by construction, we have an isomorphism

\mathcal{L}|_{U_{d}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{k}_{U_{d}}.

For
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}
, and
N
, let

\mathcal{H}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{k},N}

denote the parameter space of complete intersections of degree
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
. Let

\mathcal{I}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{H}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{k},N}

denote the tautological family of complete intersections. By Proposition
4.11
,

\RD(\mathcal{I}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{H}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{k},N})\leq\RD(i_{1}\cdots i_{k}).

By our inductive construction, we have a map

\displaystyle U_{d}

\displaystyle\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.66992pt\raise 6.68611pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.09723pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cap T_{(d+1)\cdots(d+k)}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.28061pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.56122pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{H}_{(d+1)\cdots(d+k),k}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto(T_{1\cdots d}|_{x}\times_{U_{d}}\mathcal{L}|_{U_{d}})\times_{\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{U_{d}}}T_{(d+1)\cdots(d+k)}|_{U_{d}}.

Because,
\mathcal{I}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{H}_{(d+1)\cdots(d+k),k}
is surjective, by Lemma
4.8
,

\RD(\mathcal{I}|_{U_{d}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>U_{d})\leq\RD(\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}).

Let
U_{d+1}\subset\mathcal{I}|_{U_{d}}
be a rational multi-section of
\mathcal{I}|_{U_{d}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>U_{d}
such that

\RD(U_{d+1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>U_{d})\leq\RD(\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}).

Then, by construction,
U_{d+1}
carries a canonical map

U_{d+1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>T_{1\cdots(d+k)}

making it a rational multi-section of the Tschirnhaus complete intersection. Further, by the above construction and Lemma
4.10
,

\displaystyle\RD(U_{d+1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathbf{a}})

\displaystyle\leq\max\{\RD(\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}),\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}})\}.

By assumption,
n\geq\FW(r)=\Phi(d,k)\geq\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}+1
. Lemma
5.5
thus implies that
\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}\geq\FW(r-1)
. Therefore, by the inductive hypothesis,

\RD(\frac{(d+k)!}{d!})\leq\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}-(r-1).

Moreover, from the definition of
\Phi(d,k)
,
n\geq\Phi(d,k)
implies that
n\geq\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}})+r
.

By Lemma
4.12
, we therefore conclude that

\displaystyle\RD(n)

\displaystyle\leq\max\{\RD(U_{d+1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathbf{a}}),\dim(\ev(T_{1\cdots(d+k)}))-1\}

\displaystyle\leq\max\{\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}-(r-1),\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}}),n-r.\}

\displaystyle=n-r.

Case 2:
n
even.
Let
U_{1}=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathbf{a}}
. By Lemma
2.7
, the family
T^{\prime}_{12}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathbf{a}}
is generically smooth. By Corollary
2.14
, there exists a dense open
V\subset Gr(T^{\prime}_{12})
, such that

\mathcal{L}|_{V}\times_{\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathbf{a}}}}T^{\prime}_{123}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathbf{a}}

is smooth (i.e. for the generic polynomial, the intersection of
T^{\prime}_{123}(\mathbf{a})
with a generic maximal isotropic in
T^{\prime}_{12}(\mathbf{a})
is smooth).

By Corollary
5.11
,

\RD(V\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathbf{a}})=1

More precisely, there exists a multi-section
U_{2}\subset V
such that
U_{2}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>U_{1}
is a solvable cover of its image, and such that

\mathcal{L}|_{U_{2}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{\frac{n}{2}-2}_{U_{2}}.

Now, by Lemma
5.3
and our assumption on
n

\displaystyle n-1

\displaystyle\geq\Phi(d,k)\geq\psi(d,k)_{d-1}+2

\displaystyle=2\psi(d,k)_{d-2}+3.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{n}{2}-2

\displaystyle\geq\psi(d,k)_{d-2}

If
\frac{n}{2}-2=\psi(d,k)_{d-2}
, then we obtain a map

\displaystyle U_{2}

\displaystyle\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{L}|_{x}\times_{\mathbb{P}^{n-2}}T^{\prime}_{123}|_{x}.

If
\frac{n}{2}-2>\psi(d,k)_{d-2}
, by the Bertini Theorem for isotropics (Proposition
2.13
), there exists a dense open

V^{\prime}\subset Gr(\psi(d,k)_{d-2},\mathcal{L}|_{U_{2}})

such that the family of cubic hypersurfaces in
\mathbb{P}^{\psi(d,k)_{d-2}}
given by

V^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{L}|_{U_{2}}}(T^{\prime}_{123}\times_{\mathbb{P}^{n-2}_{U_{1}}}\mathcal{L}|_{U_{2}})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>U_{2}

is generically smooth. Because rational points are dense in Grassmannians, perhaps after shrinking
U_{2}
, we obtain a section
U_{2}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>V^{\prime}
. As above, we again obtain a map

\displaystyle U_{2}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.69014pt\raise 6.3978pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.3978pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cap T^{\prime}_{123}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.63866pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.27731pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}}.

Note that, from the construction above,
\RD(U_{2}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>U_{1})=1
. The remainder of the proof now proceeds exactly as in the case of
n
odd.
∎

5.2
Proof of Theorem
5.8

Proof of Theorem
5.8
.

We deduce the theorem from the following:

Claim 1
.

There exists a monotone increasing function
\rho\colon\mathbb{N}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{N}
such that

1.

for
k\geq\rho(d)
,

\displaystyle\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}+1

\displaystyle=\Phi(d,k)

\displaystyle\leq\Phi(d-1,k+1)

(i.e. both conditions hold for
k\geq\rho(d)
);

2.

for all
k<\rho(d)
, either

\Phi(d,k)>\Phi(d-1,k+1).

or

\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}+1\neq\Phi(d,k)

(i.e.
\rho(d)
is the least integer such that both conditions hold).

Granting the claim, let
\varphi(d):=\rho(d+1)+d+2
. From Definition
5.4
, we see that
\rho(3)=2
, and thus
\varphi(2)=6
. However,
\FW(5)=9
while
B(5)=25
, so we can modify
\varphi
by setting
\varphi(2):=5
as claimed. Moreover, for
r\geq\varphi(d)
, we have

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle:=(r-1)-(d+1)

\displaystyle\geq\varphi(d)-(d+2)

\displaystyle\geq\rho(d+1)

As a result,

\displaystyle\FW(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{(r-1)!}{(d+1)!}+1

and therefore,

\displaystyle B(r)/\FW(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{(r-1)!+1}{(r-1)!/(d+1)!+1}

\displaystyle\geq d!

We now prove Claim
1
by asymptotic estimates; more precisely, we show that for each
d
,
\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}})
grows polynomially in
k
, while
\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}
grows superexponentially. Precise formulas for the function
\rho
require a more detailed analysis.

Continuing to follow Notation
5.2
, we claim the following:

Claim 2
.

Fix
d
. Then as a function of
k
,

\mathcal{O}((d+k)!)\geq\max\{\mathcal{O}(\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}})),\mathcal{O}(\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d-1,k+1)_{d-3}}))\}),

where
\mathcal{O}(f)
denotes the asymptotic growth of a function
f
.

Granting the claim, we see that for
k>>d
,

\Phi(d,k)=\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}<\frac{(d+k)!}{(d-1)!}=\Phi(d-1,k+1).

Note that by definition,

\displaystyle\Phi(d,k)

\displaystyle=\max\{\frac{(d+k)!}{d!}+1,\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}})+d+k+1\}

Therefore Claim
1
follows from Claim
2
. To prove Claim
2
, recall Stirling’s formula (cf.
[
Ro55
]
)

\sqrt{2\pi}m^{m+\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{1}{12m+1}-m}\leq m!\leq\sqrt{2\pi}m^{m+\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{1}{12m}-m}

This implies that

\mathcal{O}(\ln((d+k)!))=\mathcal{O}((d+k+\frac{1}{2})\ln(d+k)).

It suffices to prove that

\max\{\mathcal{O}(\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}})),\mathcal{O}(\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d-1,k+1)_{d-3}}))\}=\mathcal{O}(k^{\alpha_{d}})

for some
\alpha_{d}
, as then

\displaystyle\max\{\mathcal{O}(\ln(\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}}))),\mathcal{O}(\ln(\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d-1,k+1)_{d-3}})))\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{d}\cdot\ln(k))

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}((d+k+\frac{1}{2})\ln(d+k))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\ln(d+k)!).

Recall that
\psi(d,k)_{0}=k
and for
i>0
,

\displaystyle\psi(d,k)_{i}

\displaystyle=\lceil\psi(d,k)_{i-1}+\binom{\psi(d,k)_{i-1}+d-(i-1)}{\psi(d,k)_{i-1}}/(\psi(d,k)_{i-1}+1)\rceil.

Therefore

\displaystyle\psi(d,k)_{i}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{(d-i+1+\psi(d,k)_{i-1})\cdots(\psi(d,k)_{i-1}+2)}{(d-i+1)!}\sim(\psi(d,k)_{i-1})^{d-i}.

Because
\psi(d,k)_{1}\sim k^{d-1}
, by induction, we obtain

\displaystyle\dim(\mathcal{H}_{d-i,\psi(d,k)_{i+1}})

\displaystyle=\binom{d-i+\psi(d,k)_{i+1}}{\psi(d,k)_{i+1}}-1

\displaystyle\sim\psi(d,k)_{i+1}^{d-i}

\displaystyle\sim k^{(d-i)\frac{(d-1)!}{(d-i-2)!}}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}})

\displaystyle\sim k^{3(d-1)!}.

By the same argument,

\displaystyle\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d-1,k+1)_{d-3}})

\displaystyle\sim(k+1)^{3(d-2)!}\sim k^{3(d-2)!},

and, thus, as functions of
k
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}((d+k)!)

\displaystyle\geq\max\{\mathcal{O}(\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d,k)_{d-2}})),\mathcal{O}(\dim(\mathcal{M}_{3,\psi(d-1,k+1)_{d-3}}))\}

as claimed.
∎

.

Appendix B
Historical Background.

‘‘The theory has been a plant of slow growth.’’
(Sylvester and Hammond, 1887
7
7

7

[
SH1887
, p. 286]
)

Tschirnhaus
[
Ts1683
]
introduced his transformationto show that
\RD(n)\leq n-3
, improving upon the linear change of variables used by the Babylonians to set the first coefficient of the general polynomial to 0. A century later, Bring
[
Br1786
]
improved this for
n=5
to show that
\RD(5)=1
. Hamilton
[
Ha1836
]
was the first to show that

\lim_{n\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\infty}n-\RD(n)=\infty.

More precisely, he showed the existence a monotone increasing function
H\colon\mathbb{N}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{N}
, such that
n-\RD(n)\geq r
for
n\geq H(r)
.
8
8

8

The numbers
H(r)
are listed as the “Hamilton numbers” in the
Online Encyclopedia of Integer Sequences
.
Hamilton computed the initial values of
H
(for
r\leq 7
). Five decades later, Sylvester
[
Sy1887
]
extended Hamilton’s computations to give:

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c|c|c|c}r&4&5&6&7&8&9\\
\hline\cr H(r)&5&11&47&923&409,619&83,763,206,255\end{array}

Sylvester then sharpened Hamilton’s bounds slightly (see
[
Sy1887
, p. 485]
)
9
9

9

Writing
S(r)
for Sylvester’s sharpening, the initial values are
S(4)=5
,
S(5)=10
,
S(6)=44
,
S(7)=905
.
, and Sylvester and Hammond
[
SH1887
]
,
[
SH1888
]
gave a generating function for
H
.

Preceding Sylvester (and apparently unbeknownst to him at the time of
[
SH1887
]
), Klein
[
Kl1871
]
initiated a new approach to solving polynomials, linking it with group theory, representation theory, projective geometry, classical invariant theory, and the theory of elliptic and automorphic functions. Fundamental to Klein’s vision was the goal of reducing a given algebraic function to a simplest possible ‘‘normal form’’, with the ideal being a normal form given by the action of the monodromy group of the function on a projective space of minimal dimension.
10
10

10

As Wiman proved, this program cannot produce a solution in
\RD(n)
variables for the general degree
n
polynomial once
n
is at least
8
.
For
n=5,6,7
, this program allowed Klein
[
Kl1884
,
Kl1887
,
Kl1905
]
to reproduce the Bring/Hamilton bounds of
\RD(n)\leq n-4
with substantial simplifications in both the algebra of the formulas and the geometry of the normal forms involved. Klein also popularized the problem of finding simplest solutions of polynomials
[
Kl1908
, Second Part, Ch. II]
, was the first, or among the first, to explicitly consider the problem of lower bounds for
\RD

[
Kl1894
,
Kl1905
]
, and worked, over a 50 year span, to anchor this problem firmly within the central mathematical concerns of his time (see also
[
Kl1888
,
Kl1879
]
, and more generally
[
Kl22
,
Fr26
]
).

In his 1900 address at the Universal Exposition in Paris, Hilbert
[
Hi1900
, Problem 13]
explicitly posed the problem of the non-existence of 2-variable formulas for the general degree 7 polynomial. Hilbert’s address cements two decisive shifts for the problem: first, he explicitly called attention to the question of lower bounds on resolvent degree, made conjectures as to lower bounds, and advocated for this as the fundamental problem. Second, Hilbert built upon Enriques’ 1897 ICM address
[
En1897
]
by generalizing the problem to encompass formulas using analytic functions and even continuous ones; he then proved by a dimension count that the general three variable analytic function does not admit a formula in analytic functions of two or fewer variables. Hilbert returned to this problem at the end of his career in
[
Hi27
]
, where he explicitly conjectured that
\RD(6)=2
,
\RD(7)=3
,
\RD(8)=4
, and then sketched a beautiful geometric idea to lower
\RD(9)
to at most 4. Shortly after, Wiman
[
Wi27
]
sketched another approach to showing
\RD(n)\leq n-5
for
n\geq 9
. As Dixmier observed
[
Di93
]
, there are gaps in both Hilbert and Wiman’s proofs due to their assuming certain forms are sufficiently generic.

Progress on the general problem of bounding
\RD(n)
stalled after Hilbert. N. Chebotarev highlighted this and related questions in his 1932 ICM address
[
Ch32
]
, and in several papers in the 1930s and 1940s
[
Ch31a
,
Ch31b
,
Ch34
,
Ch43
]
. However, by the mid-20th century, much of the 19th century work appears to have been forgotten. Segre
[
Se45
]
, building on Hilbert, provided the first rigorous proof that
\RD(n)\leq n-5
for
n\geq 9
, and proved that for
n\geq 157
,
\RD(n)\leq n-6
(n.b. Hamilton proved this for
n\geq 47
, while Sylvester proved it for
n\geq 44
). G. Chebotarev (N.’s son) worked to extend Wiman’s methods to show
\RD(n)\leq n-6
for
n\geq 21

[
Ch54
]
, but his proof is incomplete.
11
11

11

As remarked above, Chebotarev’s argument has the same gap that Dixmier
[
Di93
]
observed in Hilbert and Wiman, namely certain non-generic forms are assumed to be generic.
Segre (
loc. cit.
) conjectured that in the limit

\lim_{n\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 1.99306pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\infty}n-\RD(n)=\infty.

(i.e. precisely what Hamilton had showed over a century earlier). Brauer
[
Br45
]
and Segre each reproved this statement, but without giving effective bounds
à la
Hamilton (see also
[
Se51
]
).

In 1957, Arnold (then 19 years old) published a theorem which he described as a “complete solution of the 13th problem of Hilbert”
[
Ar57
]
. A strengthening of Arnold’s theorem, published soon after by Kolmogorov
[
Ko57
]
, states that for any continuous map
f\colon[0,1]^{n}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
, there exist continuous functions
g_{j},\varphi_{ij}\colon[0,1]\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathbb{R}
such that

f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{2n-1}g_{j}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{ij}(t_{i}))

To apply this to Hilbert’s problem, one must interpret Hilbert as having asked for an obstruction to expressing a single-valued branch of the general degree 7 polynomial as a composition of (single-valued) continuous functions of two or fewer variables. Following Arnold and Kolmogorov, work on the problem in all of its forms largely collapsed, this despite Arnold’s efforts over a four decade span
[
Ar70a
,
Ar70b
,
Ar70c
,
AS76
,
Ar99
]
to call attention to and solve Hilbert’s (still open!) thirteenth problem.
12
12

12

See also
[
Ar00
, Problems 1972-27, 1976-34, 1979-10, 1980-10, 1985-18]

In 1971, Khovanskii
[
Kh70
]
showed that if one prohibited the use of division in a formula (i.e. one only allowed ‘‘entire’’ algebraic functions), then the quintic was not solvable in 1-variable functions.
13
13

13

A late paper of Abhyankar
[
Ab95
]
, apparently unaware of Khovanskii’s result, proves the analogous theorem for the sextic.
Khovanskii emphasized that, more than anything else, this result shows the importance of division.
14
14

14

Lin has also extensively investigated what one can say for the general degree
n
polynomial if one rules out division and possibly imposes further restrictions, see the papers
[
Li73
,
Li76
,
Li96
]
.

In 1975, Brauer
[
Br75
]
gave the first rigorous definition of resolvent degree in the literature (followed soon after by Arnold and Shimura
[
AS76
]
). Brauer then proved that for
n\geq(r-1)!+1
,
\RD(n)\leq n-r
. This improves Sylvester and Hamilton’s bounds for
r\geq 7
, and for such
r
provides the best upper bound, of which we are aware, prior to this paper.

While not stricly on
\RD(n)
, McMullen’s work on iterative algorithms
[
Mc88
]
and his iterative solution of the quintic with Doyle
[
DM89
]
represent one of the major outgrowths of Arnold’s efforts to obstruct solutions of polynomials. More recently, Buhler-Reichstein’s formalization of the Kronecker-Klein resolvent problem
[
BR97
,
BR99
]
, and the broader theory of essential dimension that this given rise to, provides the closest contemporary body of work (see e.g.
[
Re10
]
,
[
Me17
]
,
[
FKW19a
]
).

The interested reader can find other discussions of the history of the problem in Sylvester and Hammond
[
SH1887
]
, in Klein
[
Kl26
]
, or more recently in the surveys by Dixmier
[
Di93
]
and Vitushkin
[
Vi04
]
. For a contemporary treatment of resolvent degree and its relation to classical problems see also
[
FW19
,
FKW19b
]
.
